154	for cleaner DIF estimation.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Excellent. The revision has addressed all three issues I flagged:

1. **FPR correction**: Line 125 now shows `0.314` instead of `~0.048` ✓
2. **Cross-subject matching added**: Lines 52-74 show cross-subject results with **ΔFPR = 0.000** across all subjects ✓ (exactly as I predicted)
3. **All 5 subjects included**: formal_logic and high_school_statistics added ✓

Key verification points:

**Cross-subject matching FPR stability** (lines 98-104): Every subject shows ΔFPR(cross) = +0.000. 

This perfectly confirms the matching variable contamination hypothesis — cross-subject matching avoids contamination because injected items are in a different subject. Looking at the cross-subject ΔTPR results at p=0.5, four out of five subjects exceed the 0.3 threshold (formal_logic, high_school_statistics, human_aging, and professional_medicine all pass), while business_ethics falls short with only +0.056 across 93 items.

Within-subject matching tells a different story — only three subjects clear the bar, with business_ethics and high_school_statistics now failing, though formal_logic and human_aging still perform well. The contrast is striking: cross-subject matching achieves 4/5 pass rate versus 3/5 for within-subject, while false positive rates remain stable across both approaches.

What's particularly interesting is formal_logic's strong showing despite having just 109 items and only five strata (~22 items per stratum), which suggests the stratification strategy is working even at smaller sample sizes.

High_school_statistics reveals the real problem — matching variable contamination is severely limiting its within-subject performance, but switching to cross-subject matching brings it above the threshold. Business_ethics remains stuck below the bar regardless of method, confirming the bottleneck is simply insufficient item count rather than the matching approach itself. The FPR comparison also clarifies something important: cross-subject baseline FPR sits at 0.416, which is actually higher than the global external FPR of 0.314, reflecting the precision loss from having fewer strata in within-subject analysis.

But there's something counterintuitive emerging — cross-subject matching is producing higher baseline FPR than within-subject matching for some subjects like human_aging and professional_medicine, which shouldn't happen if cross-subject matching is properly controlling for ability. This suggests the cross-subject total score isn't capturing within-subject ability as precisely as expected, leaving more residual DIF uncontrolled.

This echoes what I saw in plan_014 where cross-subject and within-subject calibration were nearly equivalent, but here at the subject level with semi-synthetic injection, I'm getting better ΔTPR with cross-subject matching despite the higher baseline FPR for certain subjects. The pattern is consistent enough that it's worth flagging in the follow-up. I should also double-check whether formal_logic really stratified into 5 groups with only 109 items.
```

[System 触发审查]:
**[Director 请求] Claim 强度评估——pivot 后 story 能否更强？**

Cross-subject matching ΔFPR=0.000 结果非常干净。我在考虑 pivot 后的 claim 不应该是"DIF 失败的 characterization"，而是更强的 positive framing。请评估以下 story 重构：

**旧 story**：DIF 检测污染 → 失败 → characterization of why it fails

**新 story 候选**：
1. **Cross-subject DIF 作为 benchmark quality tool**：31-38% 的 MMLU items 在模型代际间功能不稳定（temporal DIF）。这不是方法失败——这是 benchmark 的结构性发现。Cross-subject matching 提供了干净的方法论（ΔFPR=0.000）来识别这些不稳定 items。
2. **DIF 双重用途**：(a) 差异性污染检测（semi-synthetic proof, ΔTPR=0.36-0.51）+ (b) benchmark item instability 量化工具
3. **核心贡献从"检测污染"转向"benchmark psychometric health assessment"**——DIF 检测的不只是污染，而是所有导致 item 在不同 model cohort 间功能差异的因素（污染、能力提升、训练数据分布变化）

请评估：
- 这个 framing 是否比"characterization of DIF limitations"更强？
- Claims 能支撑 ICLR 2027 还是必须降到 findings track？
- 有没有我遗漏的角度可以进一步加强？

数据清单：plan_011 (semi-synthetic ΔTPR), plan_014 (within-subject 38%), plan_016 (cross-subject ΔFPR=0.000), plan_013 (Q3 diagnostics), plan_001 (enrichment≈1.0)

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — irt_equated_bench_regen\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: ICLR 2027 (expected deadline ~October 2026; 5 months for full pipeline experiments)\\n- Backup: AAAI 2027 (abstract July 21, paper July 28, 2026 — tight, 2 months)\\n- Backup 2: EACL 2027 via ARR August 2026 cycle (August 3, 2026)\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条, 可证伪)\\n\\n**Claim 1 (Detection)**: DIF analysis across model release cohorts detects benchmark contamination with practical power (ETS C: TPR 0.72-0.85, FPR <0.05 at N≥80, δ≥1.4) and is robust to RL training — because it operates on group-level behavioral response patterns (correct/incorrect conditional on ability), not within-model output distributions that GRPO/PPO smoothing affects.\\n\\n**Claim 2 (Ensemble Coverage)**: A multi-signal contamination sentinel combining DIF (large effects, RL-robust), ConStat-style perturbation testing (non-RL models), and DVD generation variance (variant contamination) achieves detection coverage across the full effect-size spectrum and model-type landscape, with each signal covering the blind spots of the others.\\n\\n**Claim 3 (Regeneration)**: Bifactor IRT equating with anchor items enables psychometrically equivalent benchmark regeneration — flagged items can be replaced with empirically calibrated alternatives that preserve construct validity, difficulty, and discrimination, maintaining benchmark comparability through a formal graceful-degradation framework.\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n**Problem**: LLM benchmarks implicitly assume temporal score comparability — when we say \\\"Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,\\\" we presume both scores reflect the same latent ability. Contamination systematically erodes this validity. Critically, RL training (GRPO/PPO) conceals contamination signals: Wang et al. (ICLR 2026) show 14-20pp AUROC degradation for all reference-free detectors after ~156 GRPO steps, reducing most to near-random (~50% AUROC). The model ecosystem is shifting toward RL-trained reasoning models (o1, o3, DeepSeek-R1), making this a growing threat.\\n\\n**Existing approaches & gaps**: LiveBench updates monthly from recent sources but applies no psychometric calibration across updates. ConStat provides FDR-controlled performance-based detection but relies on perturbation sensitivity that RL can mask. DVD detects variant contamination via generation variance but is untested against RL. Growing Pains (arXiv 2026) uses multidimensional IRT for extensible evaluation but addresses score comparability, not contamination. No existing work closes the full loop from RL-robust detection through construct-constrained generation to psychometrically equated regeneration.\\n\\n**Our framing**: Contamination is fundamentally a psychometric problem — it creates Differential Item Functioning (DIF), where contaminated items become differentially easier for exposed model cohorts beyond what general ability gains predict. DIF operates on group-level Item Characteristic Curves conditional on ability, making it orthogonal to within-model output distribution smoothing.\\n\\nA critical distinction this work reveals: **global vs differential contamination**. Existing contamination labels (web-overlap, n-gram matching) measure whether an item is publicly accessible — a static, model-agnostic property. DIF detects whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts — a dynamic, group-specific signal. When benchmark items are globally available (e.g., MMLU on Common Crawl since 2020), all models have equal exposure, producing no group-specific DIF. DIF detects differential contamination: items that entered specific models' training data at different times or through different pipelines. Semi-synthetic validation (controlled injection into real response matrices) confirms DIF achieves TPR=0.83 at 50% exploitation rate with clear dose-response, while enrichment against global web-overlap labels is ≈1.0 — as expected, not as failure. This distinction between global and differential contamination is itself a methodological contribution.\\n\\nDIF serves as a high-sensitivity screening tool (high recall) rather than a standalone diagnostic. Combined with ConStat (precision filtering) in a dual-signal ensemble, and bifactor IRT equating for calibrated item regeneration, this creates a psychometric monitoring and regeneration framework — applying mature, validated psychometric tools to solve an urgent ML evaluation problem.\\n\\n**Headline result template**: \\\"Semi-synthetic validation on the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models × 12,508 MMLU items) demonstrates that MH-DIF detects differential contamination with TPR=0.83 at 50% exploitation and TPR=1.0 at full exploitation (Fisher p<10⁻⁴²), with clear dose-response characteristics. We reveal a fundamental distinction between global contamination (web exposure, measured by existing labels) and differential contamination (group-specific training data inclusion, measured by DIF) — explaining why DIF enrichment against web-overlap labels is ≈1.0 while controlled injection yields near-perfect detection. Combined with ConStat in a dual-signal ensemble, this achieves practical screening coverage across the effect-size spectrum.\\\"\\n\\n## Venue Fit Risks\\n\\n- **ICLR**: Strong fit — evaluation methodology, robustness to RL, psychometric theory applied to ML. Risk: reviewers may not be familiar with IRT/DIF methodology. Mitigation: provide self-contained DIF tutorial in appendix, reproducible code, and connection to ML-familiar concepts (DIF ≈ \\\"fairness testing for items\\\").\\n- **Cross-discipline bridge**: IRT/psychometrics vocabulary needs translation for ML audience. Key mappings: \\\"examinees\\\" → \\\"models\\\", \\\"test items\\\" → \\\"benchmark questions\\\", \\\"DIF\\\" → \\\"item-level contamination signal\\\", \\\"equating\\\" → \\\"score comparability across benchmark versions\\\".\\n- **Novelty concern**: DIF itself is not new (1988). Our contribution is the novel application to LLM benchmark contamination + the RL-robustness argument + the integration with regeneration. Need to frame as \\\"bringing mature psychometric tools to solve an urgent ML problem\\\" rather than \\\"inventing new statistics.\\\"\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns\\n\\n1. **\\\"DIF power is only sufficient for large effects (δ≥1.4)\\\"**\\n   - Response: Large effects are precisely what threaten benchmark validity most (GSM1K: 10-13% average accuracy drop). Small effects (δ<0.6) have minimal impact on rankings. For medium effects, the ensemble's other signals provide coverage.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_001 (real-data validation), plan_003 (ensemble)\\n\\n2. **\\\"Model cohort sample sizes are too small for classical DIF\\\"**\\n   - Response: (a) ETS C uses effect-size thresholds, not p-values, reducing sample-size dependence; (b) annual cohorts now reach N=80-100; (c) Cumulative Bayesian priors accumulate power over time; (d) Item-Focused Trees treat release date as continuous covariate.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_001 (real cohort sizes), plan_008 (Bayesian DIF, P2)\\n\\n3. **\\\"Only validated in simulation\\\"**\\n   - Response: plan_001 uses METABENCH real data (5,055 models). plan_004 cross-validates on GSM8K with GSM1K ground truth.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_001, plan_004\\n\\n4. **\\\"Item regeneration quality — how do you ensure replacements measure the same construct?\\\"**\\n   - Response: Bifactor IRT equating with anchor items + empirical calibration via student simulation (not prompt-based difficulty targeting). Construct validity verified through factor loading analysis.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_005, plan_006\\n\\n5. **\\\"RL robustness is only theoretical — where's the empirical evidence?\\\"**\\n   - Response: plan_002 directly evaluates DIF on cohorts including RL-trained models.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_002\\n\\n6. **\\\"Computational cost of the full pipeline\\\"**\\n   - Response: DIF analysis is lightweight (minutes, CPU only). Main cost is one-time calibration panel. Once calibrated, ongoing monitoring is cheap.\\n\\n7. **\\\"DIF flags may reflect capability improvement, not contamination\\\"**\\n   - Response: Post-2024 models are both more capable and more contaminated. We control for this via (a) within-family DIF analysis (same architecture at different training stages, e.g., Llama-2→3→3.1), where capability trajectory is known, isolating contamination-specific DIF; (b) domain-specificity analysis of DIF flags vs known capability trajectories; (c) bifactor model's subject-specific factors as covariates to absorb domain-specific capability gains.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_001 (within-family sub-analysis), plan_005 (bifactor covariates)\\n\\n8. **\\\"Ground truth is too noisy to validate DIF\\\"**\\n   - Response: Three-layer validation strategy. (a) Li et al. (EMNLP 2024) web-overlap labels as proxy — but we frame correctly: Li et al. measures *existence* contamination (item on web), DIF measures *differential* contamination (item behaves differently across cohorts). Low concordance is expected and explained, not a failure. FPR on Li-clean items is the primary metric. (b) Semi-synthetic injection: controlled ground truth with known contamination injected into real response matrix. (c) GSM8K/GSM1K cross-benchmark validation with independent ground truth.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_001 (Li et al. proxy), plan_011 (semi-synthetic), plan_004 (GSM8K)\\n\\n9. **\\\"Partial exploitation defeats DIF — RL models may only exploit p<1 of memorized items\\\"**\\n   - Response: Formal power analysis as function of exploitation rate p shows: at p=1.0 DIF detects with TPR=0.72-0.85; at p=0.6 effect size shrinks ~0.6×, falling to medium range where ensemble's ConStat signal provides coverage. DIF + ensemble jointly cover p≥0.4. Below p=0.4, contamination impact on rankings is minimal.\\n   - Evidence needed: plan_002 (partial exploitation analysis)\\n\", \"total_chars\": 9682, \"truncated\": false}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {}}"}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MDJyTeVmisw4v6fDVgLYVV`</summary>

```text
1	---
2	name: negative-framing-review
3	description: "当 Director 转发 Agent 的 Framing 调整提案给你审查时,立即使用本 skill。四维度审查:rationalization 检测 / Evidence 对齐 / Narrative 连贯 / 诚实度。任一不过关即 critical 打回。防 Agent 把 negative 结果包装成'重要发现'。"
4	always: false
5	roles:
6	  - reviewer
7	---
8	
9	# Negative Framing 独立审查
10	
11	Director 转发 Agent 调 red-section 产出的"Framing 调整提案"给你独立审查。你的职责是防止 Agent **rationalize**（把所有 negative 结果都包装成"重要发现"）。
12	
13	## 四维度审查（任一不过关即 critical）
14	
15	### 1. Rationalization 检测
16	- 原 hypothesis 是否被偷换?（检查 ROADMAP / idea.yaml 的原始 claim vs 新 framing 的 claim）
17	- 是否把"method 不 work"硬说成"我们发现了 method 的 limitation"?
18	- 新 framing 的贡献声明在现有实验数据下真的成立,还是只换了说法掩盖弱结果?
19	
20	### 2. Evidence 对齐
21	- 新 framing 的每个 claim 是否有 registry 中的实验证据?
22	- 有没有现有实验**反驳**新 framing 而被忽略?
23	- 新 framing 需要的 ablation / 补实验是否已经识别出来?
24	
25	### 3. Narrative 连贯性
26	- abstract / intro / contribution / experiments / conclusion 在新 framing 下是否自洽?
27	- 有没有遗留旧 framing 的 claim 尚未清理?
28	
29	### 4. 诚实度
30	- limitations section 是否承认了这次调整的代价?
31	- related work 定位是否需要重写（新 framing 可能对标不同的 SOTA）?
32	
33	## 输出
34	
35	`reviewer_post_to_director` 附具体证据段落。
36	- critical → 提案必须重做,Agent 不得直接改 paper
37	- warning → 允许进入下一步,但必须修正指出的问题
38	- info → 通过
39	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the negative-framing-review skill loaded. Let me do the four-dimensional review of the Director's proposed reframing.

## Context Review

**Original hypothesis/claims** (from paper_framing.md):
- Claim 1 (Detection): DIF detects contamination with TPR 0.72-0.85, FPR <0.05
- Claim 2 (Ensemble): Multi-signal contamination sentinel combining DIF + ConStat + DVD
- Claim 3 (Regeneration): Bifactor IRT equating with anchor items for benchmark regeneration
  
**What actually happened** (experiments D001-D010):
Looking at the experimental results, DIF failed to distinguish contaminated from clean items in temporal and matched-ability conditions with enrichment scores near 1.0, but showed promise in semi-synthetic injection scenarios with a substantial TPR improvement. However, conditioning on ability and other refinements didn't resolve the persistent false positive rate around 31%, revealing fundamental limitations in the detection approach.

**Key finding**: DIF appears fundamentally confounded with ability improvement in temporal cohorts—the 31-38% of items showing C-level DIF likely reflects genuine model generational differences rather than actual contamination.

**Four-Dimensional Review**

Now examining whether the proposed narratives are rationalizations or genuine insights. The first candidate frames cross-subject DIF as a benchmark quality tool, suggesting that the 31-38% item instability across cohorts represents a valid methodology for detecting functional differences rather than contamination artifacts.

This feels like a mixed case. The original goal was contamination detection, but the experiments revealed DIF captures all item-level differences between cohorts, not specifically contamination. Reframing this as "item instability is interesting even if we can't isolate contamination" does shift the goalposts somewhat. However, the finding itself is substantive—MMLU items genuinely aren't functioning consistently across model generations, which has real implications for benchmark validity beyond just contamination concerns. The cross-subject methodology with zero false positive rate across subjects is also a legitimately clean measurement approach. The question becomes whether 38% temporal instability is actually meaningful for benchmark quality assessment.

The dual-use framing—treating this as both contamination detection and item instability quantification—seems more honest, but there's a real problem: the contamination detection was only validated semi-synthetically, never against actual contamination in the wild. The enrichment against existing labels was near-perfect, but that just confirms the methodology works as designed, not that it catches real contamination. So I'm left with a tool that measures item instability well but has no real-world validation for its original purpose.

The most defensible claim is probably the simplest: this is a psychometric health assessment showing which items function differently across cohorts. It's descriptive rather than prescriptive, which feels weaker than "we can detect contamination," but at least it's honest about what the evidence actually supports. The original framing had a clear action—find contamination, regenerate items—but this new framing leaves the "so what?" question hanging.

Looking at what I can actually claim: the 31-38% instability rate is solid across multiple validation approaches, the cross-subject matching methodology is clean with zero false positive rate, and the DIF detection works on semi-synthetic contamination. But that last one is the sticking point—semi-synthetic isn't real-world.

The bigger problem is that I have no actual evidence that DIF detects contamination in real items. The enrichment analysis shows DIF doesn't correlate with any known contamination signal, and while I can explain that away with a "global vs differential" distinction, that's unfalsifiable without ground truth. Plus the original claims about ensemble methods and item regeneration were never tested at all. Even the "benchmark quality tool" framing that suggests removing unstable items—removing 38% of the test is a pretty aggressive action to take without stronger validation.

Looking at the narrative structure, the old framing still hinges on three claims that I've essentially abandoned two of. The new story candidates each have different problems: Candidate 1 is clean and coherent (instability detected, benchmark validity questioned) but purely descriptive with no solution. Candidate 2 tries to hold both contamination detection and instability characterization together, which creates an awkward tension where I'm saying "we can detect contamination in controlled settings but real-world DIF is probably just ability differences." It's honest but confusing.

The core limitation I need to front-load is that DIF alone can't separate contamination from genuine capability gains without ground truth, and my semi-synthetic validation only proves one specific contamination mechanism works. The 38% temporal instability could be either, or both, and I have no way to tell. Plus my cross-subject baseline still has a 33-53% false positive rate—I've shown it's stable, not that it's actually reliable. And I'm not delivering any practical tool, just characterization of the problem.

For ICLR 2027, applying textbook psychometric methods to LLMs is a novel application but probably not novel enough methodologically. The 38% instability finding is concerning but feels incomplete without actionable solutions—reviewers will want to know what to do about it. The paper reads as a negative result: standard methods don't catch contamination, items are unstable, but here's no path forward.

For EMNLP/ACL Findings, this lands better as solid empirical characterization work. The semi-synthetic framework and the distinction between global versus differential contamination feel like genuine conceptual contributions for the NLP community.

I'm leaning toward Findings track as the realistic target. To reach ICLR main, they'd need something more substantial—either a working detector validated on real data, a theoretical impossibility result, or a practical tool that addresses the detection problem.

Now I'm weighing whether the reframing strategies are honest insights or just rationalization. The benchmark quality tool angle works best because it doesn't oversell contamination detection, though it needs to acknowledge that item instability could reflect genuine capability improvements rather than contamination. The dual-use framing is more candid but harder to pitch. The psychometric health assessment feels too nebulous.

The real risk is treating 38% temporal DIF as a structural finding when it might just reflect that newer models perform better on certain items—which is expected and not particularly novel. The key question is whether this work actually reveals something meaningful about benchmarks or just documents normal model improvement.

Looking at the correlation between DIF and accuracy gains, the domain specificity is weak enough that it suggests the findings are largely expected—items where models improved most show the most DIF, which is trivial. This feels like a warning sign that the reframing is stretching the significance. The work has genuine elements but risks overstating what it shows, so ICLR main conference is probably too ambitious; a findings track would be more fitting.
```

</details>

exp_id: dream_20260520T205007\\\\n    role: dream_simulation\\\\n    result: best=3/6, median=3-4/6, worst=3/6\\\\n  depends_on:\\\\n  - D008\\\\n  - D007\\\\n  - D006\\\\n- id: D010\\\\n  date: 2026-05-20 22:29 UTC\\\\n  summary: 方向 3 (Bifactor) pre-check 否定：within-subject temporal C%=38.1% (range 31-45%)，高于\\\\n    overall 31%。Bifactor 移除的 between-subject variance 反而在压低 C%。三种结构性改进方向全部失败，Method→Analysis\\\\n    pivot 前提 (a)(b) 已满足\\\\n  rationale: 'plan_014 within-subject DIF 前置检查：5 个 subject 的 within-subject temporal\\\\n    C% 平均 38.1%，没有一个低于 30%。Cross-subject matching 仅 Δ=-2.4pp。这证明 temporal DIF 是 item-specific\\\\n    的真实差异（能力提升+污染+其他），不是 matching artifact。Bifactor 即使完美运行也无法降 FPR 到 15% 以下。决策链：D005(方向1失败)\\\\n    → D008(方向2失败) → D010(方向3 pre-mortem 证伪)。Oracle: plan_011 semi-synthetic ΔTPR=+0.508\\\\n    确认方法本身有效。Pivot 前提 (c) 待用户确认投稿目标调整。'\\\\n  related_experiments:\\\\n  - <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Interesting — D011 has been added since my last review. The Director has already made a decision (D011) to pivot and KEEP ICLR 2027 target. Let me analyze:

D011 summary: "Pivot 拍板：从'DIF 检测方法'转向'LLM benchmark psychometric characterization + DIF methodology'。Cross-subject matching ΔFPR=0.000 是方法论突破，31-38% temporal DIF 是实证发现。保持 ICLR 2027 目标不降级"


Now I'm evaluating the three candidate reframings against D011, which appears to favor a hybrid of Candidate 1/3 while maintaining the ICLR 2027 timeline. Looking at the methodological claims more carefully though — the cross-subject matching with zero FPR difference is being positioned as a novel contribution, but this approach of using external scores for MH-DIF isn't actually new to the field.

The temporal DIF analysis across LLM benchmarks at this scale does seem genuinely novel though — I haven't seen large-scale DIF quantification on LLM benchmarks before, so that claim holds up better. The Director's preference to keep ICLR 2027 is their call, but I need to assess whether the evidence actually supports a main conference paper at that venue.

Let me work through the four-dimensional review to check for rationalization in the original claims.

The core issue is that Claim 1 about contamination detection doesn't match what actually happened. The DIF method is picking up all temporal differences (31-38% of items), not contamination specifically, and the false positive rate is 31% — nowhere near the <0.05 threshold we'd need. The semi-synthetic validation shows the method can work for differential contamination with a strong TPR boost, but real-world results show enrichment around 1.0, which doesn't validate the contamination hypothesis. Claim 2 about the multi-signal ensemble was never tested, and Claim 3 about bifactor IRT regeneration got dropped in D010. So the new framing is trying to reposition the 31-38% FPR as a positive finding about item instability rather than acknowledging it as a methodological limitation.

The question of whether this is rationalization hinges on whether "31-38% of items show functional instability across model generations" is genuinely useful for the community. There's something real there — knowing MMLU items drift across model versions does matter for benchmark design. But the reframing only works if we can answer what should actually be done about it. The original Claim 3 proposed regenerating unstable items, but that got abandoned.

The cross-subject matching using external scores is standard psychometric practice, so calling ΔFPR=0.000 a methodological breakthrough is overselling what's essentially a validation check we already knew would work. The detection trilemma is the shakiest reframing — characterizing three failed approaches as a fundamental tradeoff is textbook post-hoc framing unless we can formally prove the tradeoff is actually fundamental rather than just an artifact of our specific methods. Right now it's just an empirical observation that we couldn't solve the problem.

On the first dimension, there's rationalization happening, but the benchmark quality tool claim has enough real substance that it's not purely post-hoc. The critical thing is being honest about what we actually found versus what we expected to find.

Now I'm looking at how well the evidence supports each claim. The temporal instability finding is solid across multiple experiments, and the cross-subject matching result is clean. The differential contamination detection only works in semi-synthetic settings though, which is a real limitation. The trilemma evidence is still fragmentary.

The bigger problem is what's missing entirely. There's no actual ground truth for differential contamination in real data, so the global versus differential distinction is really just a story we're telling after the fact to explain why plan_001 failed. And for the benchmark quality tool idea—we haven't actually shown that removing flagged items makes benchmarks better or more stable. The trilemma doesn't have a formal proof either, just three failed approaches. There's also a contradiction lurking in the domain specificity correlation that suggests DIF might not be as universal as we're claiming.

Now looking at the narrative structure, the old framing document is built on three claims that are all either falsified or never tested. The whole thing needs to be rewritten around what we actually found: LLM benchmarks get used for temporal comparisons, but items function differently across model generations. We applied psychometric DIF methods to measure this, found that 31-38% of items show temporal DIF, and showed that cross-subject matching gives stable estimates. That's the real story, and it needs to replace the old claims entirely.

The contributions are solid—this is the first large-scale psychometric look at LLM benchmark stability, we've built a semi-synthetic validation framework for DIF methods, and we've clarified the distinction between global and differential contamination. Whether that's interesting enough is the real question.

But I need to be direct about what we can't claim: DIF can't tell us whether those 38% of items changed because models got better or because they saw the data before. We didn't actually detect real contamination in the wild—our enrichment was basically 1.0 against the only labels we had. The semi-synthetic setup uses one specific contamination mechanism that might not match how things actually happen. The cross-subject matching baseline still has 33-53% false positive rate, which is stable but honestly pretty high. And we've only tested this on MMLU and METABENCH, so we don't know if it generalizes across other benchmarks.

For positioning, I need to reframe the related work—Pains and METABENCH aren't competitors, they're doing complementary things. The contamination detection literature should be repositioned the same way. And this is applied DIF work, so I need to properly situate it against the educational testing literature where DIF actually comes from.

Thinking about ICLR 2027: the novel application of psychometrics to LLMs is interesting, the scale is real (5227 models, 12508 items), the methodology is clean, and the global versus differential contamination distinction is a genuine conceptual contribution. But the limitations are substantial enough that I'm honestly not sure this lands as a 6-7 or a 5-6.

The core issue is that I'm characterizing a problem without solving it—no working system, no clear guidance for practitioners beyond "38% of items are unstable," and the detection trilemma is empirical rather than a formal result. Plus I'm only working with MMLU through METABENCH. The psychometric methods themselves are standard; the novelty is purely in applying them to LLMs.

To get this to a 6-7 at ICLR, I'd need either cross-benchmark validation like GSM8K, a formal impossibility result around the detection trilemma, or downstream evidence that removing DIF items actually changes model behavior in meaningful ways. Without one of those, Findings Track at ACL or EMNLP is probably the safer target where this lands more solidly at 6-7.

But there's something I might be overlooking: the semi-synthetic validation framework itself could be the real contribution if I reframe it as a general methodology for stress-testing contamination detection methods rather than just proving DIF works. That's actually the strongest methodological angle here.
```

benchmark 验证 |\n| Cross-matching FPR 稳定 | plan_016 ΔFPR=0.000 | ✅ 强 | 但 baseline FPR 仍 33-53% |\n| DIF 检测差异性污染 | plan_011 ΔTPR=+0.508 | ⚠️ 仅 semi-synthetic | 无真实差异性污染 ground truth |\n| Global vs differential 区分 | plan_001 enrichment≈1.0 + plan_011 ΔTPR>0 | ⚠️ Post-hoc 解释 | 理论合理但 unfalsifiable |\n| Detection trilemma | D005/D008/D010 三连败 | ⚠️ 经验模式 | 无 formal proof |\n\n**被忽略的反向证据**：plan_001 domain specificity ρ=0.307 (C_rate vs ΔAcc, p=0.02) 表明 DIF 与准确率提升正相关。被标记为\"不稳定\"的 items 很大程度上就是模型进步最大的 items。**\"item instability\" 可能只是 \"models got better\" 的另一种说法** —— 这在论文中必须诚实讨论。\n\n---\n\n### 维度 3: Narrative 连贯性\n\n**三个候选中 Candidate 1（benchmark quality tool）最强**，原因：\n- 自洽叙事：problem (benchmark temporal comparability) → method (cross-subject DIF) → finding (38% unstable) → implication (benchmark validity concern)\n- 不需要声称解决了污染检测问题\n- Semi-synthetic framework 作为方法论附加贡献\n\n**Candidate 2（dual use）的内在张力**：\"我们能检测注入的污染（semi-synthetic proof）但真实数据上全是能力提升\"——honest but confusing for readers。\n\n**Candidate 3（psychometric health assessment）太泛**，缺乏 actionable output。\n\n**残留旧 framing**：paper_framing.md 仍写着 Claim 1-3（detection FPR<0.05, ensemble, regeneration），全部需要重写。\n\n---\n\n### 维度 4: 诚实度\n\n**必须在 limitations 中承认的**:\n1. DIF 无法区分污染与能力提升（38% 包含两者，无法分解）\n2. 未实现真实世界污染检测（enrichment≈1.0，global vs differential 是 post-hoc 解释）\n3. Semi-synthetic 的注入机制（incorrect→correct flipping）可能不匹配真实污染模式\n4. 只在 MMLU/METABENCH 上验证，无跨 benchmark 验证\n5. \"Benchmark quality tool\" 无 downstream 验证——未证明移除 DIF items 改善评估质量\n6. Cross-subject baseline FPR 33-53%——稳定但不低\n\nRelated work 需要重新定位：Growing Pains (2026), METABENCH (ICLR 2025) 从\"不同问题\"变成直接相关工作。\n\n---\n\n### ICLR 2027 vs Findings Track 评估\n\n**对 ICLR 2027 主会的坦诚评估**：\n\n现有证据支撑一篇 5-6 分的 characterization paper（solid but incremental）。ICLR reviewer 会问：\"So what? 38% items unstable, what do we do about it?\" 无 actionable output 是最大短板。\n\n**要到 ICLR 主会 6-7 分，至少需要以下之一**：\n1. **Cross-benchmark 验证**（plan_004 GSM8K/GSM1K 有独立 ground truth）→ 最值得做\n2. **Downstream impact 实验**：移除 DIF items 后模型排名是否更稳定？benchmark reliability 是否改善？\n3. **Detection trilemma formal result**：证明为什么 1D conditioning 必然失败（不只是\"试了3个都不行\"）\n\n**Findings track (EMNLP/ACL)**：6-7 分，solid empirical contribution + methodological framework。几乎确定 accept。\n\n**注意**：venue 决策不在我的职责范围内。以上是 evidence sufficiency 评估，不是投稿建议。\n\n---\n\n### 你可能遗漏的角度\n\n**Semi-synthetic validation framework 作为独立贡献**：不要只把它当 proof-of-concept。framing 为\"a reusable framework for validating any contamination detection method on real response matrices\"——任何新的 detection method 都可以用这个框架做 controlled validation。这是 infrastructure contribution，比\"DIF works\"更 general。\n\n**反向利用 enrichment≈1.0**：现有 contamination labels（Li et al., n-gram overlap 等）在 LLM 场景下到底在测什么？enrichment≈1.0 结合 plan_011 的 positive semi-synthetic proof 说明：现有\"污染标签\"实际上**不标注差异性污染**。如果 framing 为\"我们发现现有污染检测 ground truth 自身有根本性缺陷\"，这是对整个 contamination detection 领域的 meta-contribution——比\"DIF 能/不能检测污染\"更有 impact。",
  "project": "irt_equated_bench_regen"
}

2	  "raw": "文献调查已完成，写入 `artifacts/literature_survey_d011.md` 并注册。\n\n覆盖 19 篇文献，5 个类别：\n\n1. **DIF 经典** (4篇): Shealy & Stout 1993 (SIBTEST), Clauser & Mazor 1998 (方法综述), Zumbo 1999 (logistic regression 框架), Holland & Wainer 1993 (教科书)\n2. **IRT in NLP** (5篇): Lalor et al. 2016/2019, Vania et al. 2021, Rodriguez et al. 2021, Polo et al. 2024 (tinyBenchmarks)\n3. **污染检测** (6篇): Shi et al. 2024 (Min-K%), Zhang et al. 2024 (Min-K%++), Balloccu et al. 2024, Golchin & Surdeanu 2024, Oren et al. 2024, Deng et al. 2024\n4. **动态 Benchmark** (5篇): DyVal 1/2, LiveBench, MMLU-Pro, MMLU-Redux\n5. **关键对标** (2篇): Growing Pains (详细技术对比表) 和 METABENCH\n\nGrowing Pains 关键发现：他们用 MIRT-2PL + Fixed Parameter Calibration 做高效评估，但**不做 DIF 分析、不检测污染**。他们在 limitations 中承认 \"anchors may become contaminated over time\" 但没有解决方案——这恰好是我们论文的核心贡献点。"
3	}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01131x5xsVw3cMVnJfKFSuaa`</summary>

```text
1	# Literature Survey for Related Work (D011)
2	
3	## 1. Impact-vs-DIF Classics
4	
5	### Shealy & Stout (1993)
6	- **Title**: A Model-Based Standardization Approach that Separates True Bias/DIF from Group Ability Differences and Detects Test Bias/DTF as Well as Item Bias/DIF
7	- **Year**: 1993
8	- **Venue**: Psychometrika, 58(2), 159–194
9	- **Key method/finding**: Developed the SIBTEST procedure — a nonparametric, IRT model-based method that detects and estimates DIF without requiring item response function (IRF) estimation. Uses a multidimensional IRT model to explain how individual items combine to produce differential test functioning (DTF) at the test level. Employs a regression correction technique to control inflated Type I error while separating true bias from group ability differences.
10	- **Relevance to our paper**: SIBTEST is the foundational statistical test for detecting item-level bias across groups. Our DIF-based contamination detection adapts this logic: instead of demographic groups, we compare "pre-contamination" vs. "post-contamination" model cohorts. The multidimensional DIF framework directly informs our approach to separating genuine ability differences from item-specific anomalies.
11	
12	### Clauser & Mazor (1998)
13	- **Title**: Using Statistical Procedures to Identify Differentially Functioning Test Items
14	- **Year**: 1998
15	- **Venue**: Educational Measurement: Issues and Practice, 17(1), 31–44
16	- **Key method/finding**: Comprehensive review of DIF detection methods including Mantel-Haenszel, logistic regression, and SIBTEST. Documents the landscape of statistical techniques for detecting items that function differently across groups while controlling for overall ability.
17	- **Relevance to our paper**: Provides the methodological taxonomy we draw from when selecting DIF detection procedures for the LLM benchmark setting. Establishes that multiple complementary DIF methods exist with different sensitivity profiles — motivating our multi-method approach.
18	
19	### Zumbo (1999)
20	- **Title**: A Handbook on the Theory and Methods of Differential Item Functioning (DIF): Logistic Regression Modeling as a Unitary Framework for Binary and Likert-Type (Ordinal) Item Scores
21	- **Year**: 1999
22	- **Venue**: Technical report, Directorate of Human Resources Research and Evaluation, Department of National Defense, Ottawa, Canada
23	- **Key method/finding**: Presents logistic regression as a unifying framework for DIF analysis applicable to both binary and ordinal item scores. Provides theoretical grounding for understanding DIF as a violation of measurement invariance.
24	- **Relevance to our paper**: Our logistic-regression-based DIF detection for binary (correct/incorrect) LLM responses directly follows this framework. Zumbo's formalization of uniform vs. non-uniform DIF maps onto our distinction between items that are uniformly easier for contaminated models vs. items showing interaction effects with model ability.
25	
26	### Holland & Wainer (1993)
27	- **Title**: Differential Item Functioning (edited volume)
28	- **Year**: 1993
29	- **Venue**: Lawrence Erlbaum Associates, Hillsdale, NJ
30	- **Key method/finding**: Definitive reference volume on DIF. Covers methods for detecting test items that function differently across examinee groups, including Mantel-Haenszel, IRT-based methods, and standardization approaches. Establishes the conceptual distinction between item impact (legitimate group differences) and item bias (construct-irrelevant difficulty).
31	- **Relevance to our paper**: The impact-vs-bias distinction is the conceptual backbone of our work. We argue that contamination-inflated performance is analogous to construct-irrelevant easiness — a form of DIF where the "group" variable is exposure to benchmark data rather than demographics.
32	
33	---
34	
35	## 2. IRT in NLP/LLM Evaluation
36	
37	### Lalor, Wu & Yu (2016)
38	- **Title**: Building an Evaluation Scale using Item Response Theory
39	- **Year**: 2016
40	- **Venue**: EMNLP 2016, pp. 648–657
41	- **Key method/finding**: First to propose IRT as an alternative means for gold-standard test-set generation and NLP system evaluation. Demonstrates that IRT can describe characteristics of individual items (difficulty, discriminating power) and account for these in estimating NLP system ability. Applied to Recognizing Textual Entailment.
42	- **Relevance to our paper**: Pioneering work establishing that IRT is applicable to NLP evaluation. Our work extends this foundation from simple item characterization to cross-cohort equating and DIF detection.
43	
44	### Lalor, Wu & Yu (2019)
45	- **Title**: Learning Latent Parameters without Human Response Patterns: Item Response Theory with Artificial Crowds
46	- **Year**: 2019
47	- **Venue**: EMNLP-IJCNLP 2019, Hong Kong
48	- **Key method/finding**: Addresses the bottleneck of needing human response patterns to calibrate IRT models by proposing to learn IRT parameters from "artificial crowds" of DNN models. Finds medium-to-large positive correlations between parameters learned from human vs. machine response patterns for NLI and sentiment analysis.
49	- **Relevance to our paper**: Validates that IRT parameters estimated from machine (model) responses are meaningful and correlate with human-derived parameters. This justifies our use of LLM response matrices for IRT calibration.
50	
51	### Vania et al. (2021)
52	- **Title**: Comparing Test Sets with Item Response Theory
53	- **Year**: 2021
54	- **Venue**: ACL 2021
55	- **Key method/finding**: Evaluated 29 datasets using predictions from 18 pretrained Transformer models via IRT. Found that Quoref, HellaSwag, and MC-TACO best distinguish among SOTA models, while SNLI, MNLI, and CommitmentBank are saturated. Provides a principled way to compare the discriminative quality of benchmark datasets.
56	- **Relevance to our paper**: Demonstrates IRT-based benchmark comparison at the dataset level. Our work extends this to item-level analysis across temporal model cohorts, using DIF to detect which specific items have degraded discriminative quality due to contamination.
57	
58	### Rodriguez et al. (2021)
59	- **Title**: Evaluation Examples Are Not Equally Informative: How Should That Change NLP Leaderboards?
60	- **Year**: 2021
61	- **Venue**: ACL-IJCNLP 2021
62	- **Key method/finding**: Creates a Bayesian IRT leaderboard model where latent subject skill and latent item difficulty predict correct responses. Demonstrates that IRT can guide annotation priorities, identify annotation errors, detect overfitting, and identify the most informative evaluation examples. Released open-source IRT leaderboard code.
63	- **Relevance to our paper**: Directly relevant methodological predecessor. Their Bayesian IRT approach to leaderboards establishes that item-level modeling reveals evaluation quality issues invisible to aggregate scores. We build on this by using IRT-derived item parameters to detect contamination-induced parameter drift.
64	
65	### Polo et al. (2024)
66	- **Title**: tinyBenchmarks: Evaluating LLMs with Fewer Examples
67	- **Year**: 2024
68	- **Venue**: ICML 2024
69	- **Key method/finding**: Shows that evaluating on ~100 curated IRT-selected items per benchmark is sufficient to accurately reproduce full-benchmark performance. Released IRT-based evaluation tools and tiny versions of Open LLM Leaderboard, MMLU, HELM, and AlpacaEval 2.0. Uses clustering-based item selection to ensure coverage of the difficulty/discrimination space.
70	- **Relevance to our paper**: tinyBenchmarks demonstrates the practical value of IRT for efficient evaluation. Their item selection methodology (clustering IRT representations) is adopted by Growing Pains for anchor selection. Our DIF analysis provides a complementary diagnostic: not just which items are informative, but which items have become unreliable due to contamination.
71	
72	---
73	
74	## 3. Contamination Detection
75	
76	### Shi et al. (2024)
77	- **Title**: Detecting Pretraining Data from Large Language Models
78	- **Year**: 2024
79	- **Venue**: ICLR 2024
80	- **Key method/finding**: Proposes MIN-K% PROB — a membership inference method that identifies pretraining data by checking whether the k% of tokens with minimum probability under the LLM are unlikely enough. Introduces the WIKIMIA benchmark for evaluation. Achieves 7.4% improvement over prior methods. Applied to copyrighted book detection, contaminated example detection, and privacy auditing.
81	- **Relevance to our paper**: Min-K% operates at the instance level (is this text in the training data?) while our DIF approach operates at the item level across model populations (does this item behave differently for likely-contaminated models?). The approaches are complementary: Min-K% requires model access per instance; DIF requires only response matrices.
82	
83	### Zhang et al. (2024) — Min-K%++
84	- **Title**: Min-K%++: Improved Baseline for Detecting Pre-Training Data from Large Language Models
85	- **Year**: 2024
86	- **Venue**: ICLR 2025
87	- **Key method/finding**: Extends Min-K% by measuring how sharply peaked the likelihood is around the input (analogous to curvature), using second-order statistics motivated by the connection between maximum likelihood training and the Hessian trace via score matching. More robust than zeroth-order signals.
88	- **Relevance to our paper**: Further develops the instance-level contamination detection paradigm. Our work offers an orthogonal, population-level signal that does not require per-instance likelihood computation.
89	
90	### Balloccu et al. (2024)
91	- **Title**: Leak, Cheat, Repeat: Data Contamination and Evaluation Malpractices in Closed-Source LLMs
92	- **Year**: 2024
93	- **Venue**: EACL 2024
94	- **Key method/finding**: Systematic review of 255 papers using ChatGPT/GPT-4, documenting that over 4.7M samples from 263 datasets were exposed through the web interface. Catalogs evaluation malpractices including unfair baselines, reproducibility issues, and lack of contamination awareness.
95	- **Relevance to our paper**: Establishes the scale and severity of the contamination problem. Their documentation of widespread data leakage motivates our work: if contamination is pervasive and hard to prevent, we need methods to detect and account for it post-hoc — exactly what DIF provides.
96	
97	### Golchin & Surdeanu (2024)
98	- **Title**: Data Contamination Quiz: A Tool to Detect and Estimate Contamination in Large Language Models
99	- **Year**: 2024
100	- **Venue**: TACL 2024
101	- **Key method/finding**: Frames contamination detection as a multiple-choice quiz: given an original instance and three word-level perturbations, a contaminated model should identify the original. Achieves 92–100% accuracy across seven datasets. Detects specific contamination in AG News, WNLI, and XSum for GPT-4.
102	- **Relevance to our paper**: DCQ is a clever per-model, per-instance detection method but requires generating perturbations and querying the model. Our DIF approach is purely observational — it works from existing response matrices without additional model queries or perturbation generation.
103	
104	### Oren et al. (2024)
105	- **Title**: Proving Test Set Contamination in Black Box Language Models
106	- **Year**: 2024
107	- **Venue**: ICLR 2024
108	- **Key method/finding**: Statistical test comparing likelihood of a benchmark's canonical ordering vs. shuffled versions. Contaminated models find canonical orderings more likely due to memorized example order. Provides exact false positive guarantees without requiring access to pretraining data or model weights. Effective on models as small as 1.4B parameters with only 1000 examples.
109	- **Relevance to our paper**: Elegant black-box method with formal statistical guarantees. However, it detects dataset-level contamination (is the whole benchmark memorized?) rather than item-level contamination effects (which specific items are affected?). Our DIF approach provides item-level granularity.
110	
111	### Deng et al. (2024)
112	- **Title**: Investigating Data Contamination in Modern Benchmarks for Large Language Models
113	- **Year**: 2024
114	- **Venue**: NAACL 2024, pp. 8706–8719
115	- **Key method/finding**: Proposes two methods: a retrieval-based system to find overlaps between benchmarks and pretraining corpora (for open-source LLMs), and Testset Slot Guessing (TS-Guessing) for proprietary LLMs. Finds ChatGPT and GPT-4 achieve 52% and 57% exact match rates in guessing missing MMLU options.
116	- **Relevance to our paper**: Demonstrates contamination in the exact benchmark (MMLU) we analyze. Their TS-Guessing evidence of MMLU contamination in GPT-4 provides ground-truth validation context for our DIF-based detection.
117	
118	---
119	
120	## 4. Dynamic/Adaptive Benchmarks
121	
122	### Zhu et al. (2024a) — DyVal
123	- **Title**: DyVal: Dynamic Evaluation of Large Language Models for Reasoning Tasks
124	- **Year**: 2024
125	- **Venue**: ICLR 2024
126	- **Key method/finding**: Generates evaluation tasks at test time using graph-based complexity control to mitigate memorization. Applies graph complexity metrics to parameterize reasoning problem difficulty. Dynamically constructs evaluation instances rather than relying on static test sets.
127	- **Relevance to our paper**: Represents the "regenerate the benchmark" approach to contamination. Our work takes the complementary "detect and account for contamination in existing benchmarks" approach, which preserves historical comparability.
128	
129	### Zhu et al. (2024b) — DyVal 2
130	- **Title**: Dynamic Evaluation of Large Language Models by Meta Probing Agents
131	- **Year**: 2024
132	- **Venue**: arXiv:2402.14865
133	- **Key method/finding**: Extends DyVal with Meta Probing Agents (MPA) inspired by psychometrics. Agents automatically transform original evaluation problems targeting three cognitive abilities: language understanding, problem solving, and domain knowledge. More generalizable than task-specific dynamic generation.
134	- **Relevance to our paper**: MPA's psychometric inspiration is conceptually aligned with our IRT-based approach, but they use psychometrics to generate new items while we use it to analyze existing items for contamination signals.
135	
136	### White et al. (2024) — LiveBench
137	- **Title**: LiveBench: A Challenging, Contamination-Free LLM Benchmark
138	- **Year**: 2024
139	- **Venue**: arXiv:2406.19314
140	- **Key method/finding**: Releases new questions monthly based on recent datasets, arXiv papers, news, and IMDb synopses. All questions have verifiable, objective ground-truth answers enabling automatic scoring without LLM judges. Spans 18 tasks across 6 categories (Math, Reasoning, Data Analysis, Language, Coding, Instruction Following).
141	- **Relevance to our paper**: LiveBench's monthly refresh strategy prevents contamination prospectively. Our DIF method detects contamination retrospectively in static benchmarks. The approaches address different temporal windows: LiveBench prevents future contamination; we diagnose past contamination in widely-used benchmarks whose historical results we want to preserve.
142	
143	### Wang et al. (2024) — MMLU-Pro
144	- **Title**: MMLU-Pro: A More Robust and Challenging Multi-Task Language Understanding Benchmark
145	- **Year**: 2024
146	- **Venue**: NeurIPS 2024
147	- **Key method/finding**: Enhances MMLU with harder, reasoning-focused questions and increases answer choices from 4 to 10, reducing random guessing success. Over 12K questions across 14 domains. GPT-4o achieves only 72.6%, and the gap between GPT-4o and GPT-4-Turbo widens from 1% (MMLU) to 9% (MMLU-Pro), showing better discriminative power.
148	- **Relevance to our paper**: MMLU-Pro addresses contamination by making a harder benchmark, but doesn't diagnose which original MMLU items are compromised. Our DIF analysis can quantify exactly which MMLU items have degraded, providing evidence for why harder replacements are needed and which content areas are most affected.
149	
150	### Gema et al. (2024) — MMLU-Redux
151	- **Title**: Are We Done with MMLU?
152	- **Year**: 2024
153	- **Venue**: arXiv:2406.04127
154	- **Key method/finding**: Manual re-annotation of 5,700 MMLU questions reveals a 6.5% error rate. Error types include parsing mistakes, multiple correct answers, and missing context. Virology subset has 57% errors. Re-evaluation with corrected labels shows significant metric discrepancies and ranking shifts.
155	- **Relevance to our paper**: MMLU-Redux addresses data quality while our work addresses contamination — both are sources of measurement error. Items flagged by DIF analysis that are not contaminated may instead have quality issues, making these analyses complementary.
156	
157	---
158	
159	## 5. Key Technical Comparisons
160	
161	### Habba, Itzhak, Yehudai, Perlitz, Bandel, Shmueli-Scheuer, Choshen & Stanovsky (2026) — Growing Pains
162	- **Title**: Growing Pains: Extensible and Efficient LLM Benchmarking Via Fixed Parameter Calibration
163	- **Year**: 2026 (April)
164	- **Venue**: arXiv:2604.12843
165	- **Authors**: Eliya Habba, Itay Itzhak, Asaf Yehudai, Yotam Perlitz, Elron Bandel, Michal Shmueli-Scheuer, Leshem Choshen, Gabriel Stanovsky
166	
167	#### Method Details
168	- **IRT Model**: Multidimensional 2PL IRT (MIRT-2PL). Each item has a discrimination vector **a_i** and intercept **d_i**.
169	- **Calibration**: Fixed Parameter Calibration (FPC) — previously calibrated item parameters are held fixed; only new item parameters are estimated when new benchmarks are added.
170	- **Anchor Selection**: Clustering-based method (following Polo et al.) that distributes anchors across the difficulty/discrimination space. Uses 100 anchor items per dataset. Outperforms top-K discrimination selection.
171	- **Scale**: 400+ models, evaluated on Open LLM Leaderboard (ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA, WinoGrande) and MMLU's 57 subjects.
172	- **Performance**: Predicts full-evaluation performance within 2–3 percentage points; Spearman ρ ≥ 0.9 for ranking preservation.
173	
174	#### Technical Comparison with Our Approach
175	
176	| Dimension | Growing Pains | Our DIF Approach |
177	|-----------|---------------|------------------|
178	| **Goal** | Efficient, extensible benchmarking via FPC | Detect contamination-induced measurement distortion |
179	| **IRT model** | MIRT-2PL | 2PL (with DIF extensions) |
180	| **Core technique** | Fixed parameter calibration with anchor items | DIF analysis across temporal model cohorts |
181	| **Contamination** | Acknowledged as limitation ("anchors may become contaminated or saturated over time, requiring recalibration") but **not addressed** | Central focus — DIF detects which items are affected |
182	| **Item drift** | Not detected; assumes item parameters are stable | Explicitly models item parameter drift as DIF signal |
183	| **DIF analysis** | Not mentioned | Core methodology |
184	| **Granularity** | Dataset/benchmark level efficiency | Item-level contamination diagnostics |
185	| **Binary response** | Yes (limitation acknowledged) | Yes |
186	
187	#### Complementarity
188	Growing Pains and our work are highly complementary:
189	1. **They assume stable item parameters; we test that assumption.** Their FPC framework relies on anchor item parameters not drifting. Our DIF analysis can identify when this assumption breaks down due to contamination.
190	2. **They optimize evaluation efficiency; we diagnose evaluation validity.** Growing Pains reduces the number of items needed; we identify which items should be trusted.
191	3. **Their acknowledged limitation is our research question.** They note "anchors may become contaminated or saturated over time, requiring recalibration" but do not address it. We provide the diagnostic tool for exactly this problem.
192	4. **Integration pathway**: DIF-flagged items could be excluded from Growing Pains' anchor sets, making their FPC calibration more robust to contamination.
193	
194	### Kipnis et al. (2025) — METABENCH
195	- **Title**: MetaBench: A Sparse Benchmark for LLM Evaluation
196	- **Year**: 2025
197	- **Venue**: ICLR 2025
198	- **Key method/finding**: Distills six Open LLM Leaderboard benchmarks (ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA, WinoGrande) into <3% of their combined size using data from 5000+ LLMs. Uses Generalized Additive Models (GAM) to model subset-to-full-set relationships. Reconstructs benchmark scores with <0.9% MAE and leaderboard scores with <0.5% MAE. Provides two disjoint versions for repeated evaluation. Integrated into EleutherAI's lm-evaluation-harness.
199	- **Relevance to our paper**: METABENCH is our primary data source — we use their 5000+ model response matrices for IRT calibration and DIF analysis. Their item selection optimizes for predictive accuracy of aggregate scores; our DIF analysis adds a validity dimension by identifying which items remain measurement-invariant across model cohorts. Items selected by MetaBench that we flag as contaminated would suggest the sparse benchmark itself may be vulnerable to contamination effects.
200	
```

      "content": "# Literature Survey for Related Work (D011)\n\n## 1. Impact-vs-DIF Classics\n\n### Shealy & Stout (1993)\n- **Title**: A Model-Based Standardization Approach that Separates True Bias/DIF from Group Ability Differences and Detects Test Bias/DTF as Well as Item Bias/DIF\n- **Year**: 1993\n- **Venue**: Psychometrika, 58(2), 159–194\n- **Key method/finding**: Developed the SIBTEST procedure — a nonparametric, IRT model-based method that detects and estimates DIF without requiring item response function (IRF) estimation. Uses a multidimensional IRT model to explain how individual items combine to produce differential test functioning (DTF) at the test level. Employs a regression correction technique to control inflated Type I error while separating true bias from group ability differences.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: SIBTEST is the foundational statistical test for detecting item-level bias across groups. Our DIF-based contamination detection adapts this logic: instead of demographic groups, we compare \"pre-contamination\" vs. \"post-contamination\" model cohorts. The multidimensional DIF framework directly informs our approach to separating genuine ability differences from item-specific anomalies.\n\n### Clauser & Mazor (1998)\n- **Title**: Using Statistical Procedures to Identify Differentially Functioning Test Items\n- **Year**: 1998\n- **Venue**: Educational Measurement: Issues and Practice, 17(1), 31–44\n- **Key method/finding**: Comprehensive review of DIF detection methods including Mantel-Haenszel, logistic regression, and SIBTEST. Documents the landscape of statistical techniques for detecting items that function differently across groups while controlling for overall ability.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Provides the methodological taxonomy we draw from when selecting DIF detection procedures for the LLM benchmark setting. Establishes that multiple complementary DIF methods exist with different sensitivity profiles — motivating our multi-method approach.\n\n### Zumbo (1999)\n- **Title**: A Handbook on the Theory and Methods of Differential Item Functioning (DIF): Logistic Regression Modeling as a Unitary Framework for Binary and Likert-Type (Ordinal) Item Scores\n- **Year**: 1999\n- **Venue**: Technical report, Directorate of Human Resources Research and Evaluation, Department of National Defense, Ottawa, Canada\n- **Key method/finding**: Presents logistic regression as a unifying framework for DIF analysis applicable to both binary and ordinal item scores. Provides theoretical grounding for understanding DIF as a violation of measurement invariance.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Our logistic-regression-based DIF detection for binary (correct/incorrect) LLM responses directly follows this framework. Zumbo's formalization of uniform vs. non-uniform DIF maps onto our distinction between items that are uniformly easier for contaminated models vs. items showing interaction effects with model ability.\n\n### Holland & Wainer (1993)\n- **Title**: Differential Item Functioning (edited volume)\n- **Year**: 1993\n- **Venue**: Lawrence Erlbaum Associates, Hillsdale, NJ\n- **Key method/finding**: Definitive reference volume on DIF. Covers methods for detecting test items that function differently across examinee groups, including Mantel-Haenszel, IRT-based methods, and standardization approaches. Establishes the conceptual distinction between item impact (legitimate group differences) and item bias (construct-irrelevant difficulty).\n- **Relevance to our paper**: The impact-vs-bias distinction is the conceptual backbone of our work. We argue that contamination-inflated performance is analogous to construct-irrelevant easiness — a form of DIF where the \"group\" variable is exposure to benchmark data rather than demographics.\n\n---\n\n## 2. IRT in NLP/LLM Evaluation\n\n### Lalor, Wu & Yu (2016)\n- **Title**: Building an Evaluation Scale using Item Response Theory\n- **Year**: 2016\n- **Venue**: EMNLP 2016, pp. 648–657\n- **Key method/finding**: First to propose IRT as an alternative means for gold-standard test-set generation and NLP system evaluation. Demonstrates that IRT can describe characteristics of individual items (difficulty, discriminating power) and account for these in estimating NLP system ability. Applied to Recognizing Textual Entailment.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Pioneering work establishing that IRT is applicable to NLP evaluation. Our work extends this foundation from simple item characterization to cross-cohort equating and DIF detection.\n\n### Lalor, Wu & Yu (2019)\n- **Title**: Learning Latent Parameters without Human Response Patterns: Item Response Theory with Artificial Crowds\n- **Year**: 2019\n- **Venue**: EMNLP-IJCNLP 2019, Hong Kong\n- **Key method/finding**: Addresses the bottleneck of needing human response patterns to calibrate IRT models by proposing to learn IRT parameters from \"artificial crowds\" of DNN models. Finds medium-to-large positive correlations between parameters learned from human vs. machine response patterns for NLI and sentiment analysis.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Validates that IRT parameters estimated from machine (model) responses are meaningful and correlate with human-derived parameters. This justifies our use of LLM response matrices for IRT calibration.\n\n### Vania et al. (2021)\n- **Title**: Comparing Test Sets with Item Response Theory\n- **Year**: 2021\n- **Venue**: ACL 2021\n- **Key method/finding**: Evaluated 29 datasets using predictions from 18 pretrained Transformer models via IRT. Found that Quoref, HellaSwag, and MC-TACO best distinguish among SOTA models, while SNLI, MNLI, and CommitmentBank are saturated. Provides a principled way to compare the discriminative quality of benchmark datasets.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Demonstrates IRT-based benchmark comparison at the dataset level. Our work extends this to item-level analysis across temporal model cohorts, using DIF to detect which specific items have degraded discriminative quality due to contamination.\n\n### Rodriguez et al. (2021)\n- **Title**: Evaluation Examples Are Not Equally Informative: How Should That Change NLP Leaderboards?\n- **Year**: 2021\n- **Venue**: ACL-IJCNLP 2021\n- **Key method/finding**: Creates a Bayesian IRT leaderboard model where latent subject skill and latent item difficulty predict correct responses. Demonstrates that IRT can guide annotation priorities, identify annotation errors, detect overfitting, and identify the most informative evaluation examples. Released open-source IRT leaderboard code.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: Directly relevant methodological predecessor. Their Bayesian IRT approach to leaderboards establishes that item-level modeling reveals evaluation quality issues invisible to aggregate scores. We build on this by using IRT-derived item parameters to detect contamination-induced parameter drift.\n\n### Polo et al. (2024)\n- **Title**: tinyBenchmarks: Evaluating LLMs with Fewer Examples\n- **Year**: 2024\n- **Venue**: ICML 2024\n- **Key method/finding**: Shows that evaluating on ~100 curated IRT-selected items per benchmark is sufficient to accurately reproduce full-benchmark performance. Released IRT-based evaluation tools and tiny versions of Open LLM Leaderboard, MMLU, HELM, and AlpacaEval 2.0. Uses clustering-based item selection to ensure coverage of the difficulty/discrimination space.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: tinyBenchmarks demonstrates the practical value of IRT for efficient evaluation. Their item selection methodology (clustering IRT representations) is adopted by Growing Pains for anchor selection. Our DIF analysis provides a complementary diagnostic: not just which items are informative, but which items have become unreliable due to contamination.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Each item has a discrimination vector **a_i** and intercept **d_i**.\n- **Calibration**: Fixed Parameter Calibration (FPC) — previously calibrated item parameters are held fixed; only new item parameters are estimated when new benchmarks are added.\n- **Anchor Selection**: Clustering-based method (following Polo et al.) that distributes anchors across the difficulty/discrimination space. Uses 100 anchor items per dataset. Outperforms top-K discrimination selection.\n- **Scale**: 400+ models, evaluated on Open LLM Leaderboard (ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA, WinoGrande) and MMLU's 57 subjects.\n- **Performance**: Predicts full-evaluation performance within 2–3 percentage points; Spearman ρ ≥ 0.9 for ranking preservation.\n\n#### Technical Comparison with Our Approach\n\n| Dimension | Growing Pains | Our DIF Approach |\n|-----------|---------------|------------------|\n| **Goal** | Efficient, extensible benchmarking via FPC | Detect contamination-induced measurement distortion |\n| **IRT model** | MIRT-2PL | 2PL (with DIF extensions) |\n| **Core technique** | Fixed parameter calibration with anchor items | DIF analysis across temporal model cohorts |\n| **Contamination** | Acknowledged as limitation (\"anchors may become contaminated or saturated over time, requiring recalibration\") but **not addressed** | Central focus — DIF detects which items are affected |\n| **Item drift** | Not detected; assumes item parameters are stable | Explicitly models item parameter drift as DIF signal |\n| **DIF analysis** | Not mentioned | Core methodology |\n| **Granularity** | Dataset/benchmark level efficiency | Item-level contamination diagnostics |\n| **Binary response** | Yes (limitation acknowledged) | Yes |\n\n#### Complementarity\nGrowing Pains and our work are highly complementary:\n1. **They assume stable item parameters; we test that assumption.** Their FPC framework relies on anchor item parameters not drifting. Our DIF analysis can identify when this assumption breaks down due to contamination.\n2. **They optimize evaluation efficiency; we diagnose evaluation validity.** Growing Pains reduces the number of items needed; we identify which items should be trusted.\n3. **Their acknowledged limitation is our research question.** They note \"anchors may become contaminated or saturated over time, requiring recalibration\" but do not address it. We provide the diagnostic tool for exactly this problem.\n4. **Integration pathway**: DIF-flagged items could be excluded from Growing Pains' anchor sets, making their FPC calibration more robust to contamination.\n\n### Kipnis et al. (2025) — METABENCH\n- **Title**: MetaBench: A Sparse Benchmark for LLM Evaluation\n- **Year**: 2025\n- **Venue**: ICLR 2025\n- **Key method/finding**: Distills six Open LLM Leaderboard benchmarks (ARC, GSM8K, HellaSwag, MMLU, TruthfulQA, WinoGrande) into <3% of their combined size using data from 5000+ LLMs. Uses Generalized Additive Models (GAM) to model subset-to-full-set relationships. Reconstructs benchmark scores with <0.9% MAE and leaderboard scores with <0.5% MAE. Provides two disjoint versions for repeated evaluation. Integrated into EleutherAI's lm-evaluation-harness.\n- **Relevance to our paper**: METABENCH is our primary data source — we use their 5000+ model response matrices for IRT calibration and DIF analysis. Their item selection optimizes for predictive accuracy of aggregate scores; our DIF analysis adds a validity dimension by identifying which items remain measurement-invariant across model cohorts. Items selected by MetaBench that we flag as contaminated would suggest the sparse benchmark itself may be vulnerable to contamination effects.\n",
      "numLines": 200,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 200
    }
